Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need find all integer bases b>9 such that the number 17_b divides 97_b. Interpret numbers in base b.

Let’s represent: 17_b means digits '1' and '7' in base b: value = 1*b+7 = b+7. Note 7 is digit less than base >9; requirement digits less than base: 7< b => b>7 which is satisfied by b>9. So 17_b = b+7.

Similarly, 97_b value = 9*b + 7.

We need b+7 to divide 9b+7.

Let d = b+7. Then b = d-7.

Then 9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d -63 +7 = 9d -56.

Need d divides 9d -56. So d | 9d -56.

Since d | 9d, any divisor of d divides 9d. So d | 9d -56 implies d | 56. Because (9d-56) -9*d = -56. So d must divide 56.

Thus d is a positive integer divisor of 56 greater than? Since b>9 => d = b+7 > 16. Wait base>9, so b>=10. So d = b+7 >=17. So d must be divisor of 56 that is at least 17. Divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Which are >=17? 28 and 56. Are 28 and 56 >? Let's check: d=28 gives b = d-7 = 21. Base 21 >9 good. Need also that digits '9' and '7' exist in base 21: 9<21 true, 7<21 true.

D=56 gives b =49. base 49>9 good; digits fine.

Any other possibilities? Wait check d=28 -> b=21. Does 97_base21 reduce? Compute: 9*b+7=9*21+7=189+7=196. d=28 divides 196? 196/28=7 exactly. Good.

d=56 -> b=49: 9*49+7 = 441+7 = 448. 56 divides 448? 56*8=448. Good.

Thus bases b =21 and49.

Sum=70.

But need check if any possibilities with negative divisors? Base positive integers. So answer 70.

But we must confirm there isn't any other d such that e.g., d divides 56 but maybe b >9 but less than d? Wait d is base+7 ensures >16 so as derived. So fine.

Thus final answer =70 inside box.

Sampled message

assistant 
In base \(b\),

\[
17_b = b+7,\qquad 97_b = 9b+7 .
\]

We need \(b+7\) to divide \(9b+7\).

Let  

\[
d = b+7 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad b = d-7 .
\]

Then

\[
9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d-56 .
\]

So the divisibility condition is

\[
d \mid 9d-56 .
\]

Since \(d \mid 9d\), this implies \(d \mid (9d-56)-9d = -56\); hence \(d\) must divide \(56\).

Divisors of \(56\) are \(1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56\).
Because \(b>9\), we have \(d=b+7>16\).  
Therefore only \(d=28\) and \(d=56\) satisfy this.

Corresponding bases are

\[
b = d-7 = 
\begin{cases}
28-7 = 21,\\[4pt]
56-7 = 49 .
\end{cases}
\]

Both bases allow the digits \(1,7,9\) (since \(9< b\)), and indeed

\[
17_{21} = 28,\;\; 97_{21} = 196 = 7\times 28,
\]
\[
17_{49} = 56,\;\; 97_{49} = 448 = 8\times 56 .
\]

Hence the integer bases \(b>9\) are \(21\) and \(49\).  
Their sum is \(21+49 = 70\).

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Results

Correct Answer: 70

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(28,0)
\]

and put the point \(C=(x,y)\).  
Since \(AC=91\),

\[
x^{2}+y^{2}=91^{2}=8281 \qquad (1)
\]

The points on \(AC\) are

\[
F=\frac{13}{91}C=\Big(\frac{13x}{91},\frac{13y}{91}\Big),\qquad   
G=\frac{65}{91}C=\Big(\frac{65x}{91},\frac{65y}{91}\Big)
\]

The reflections are  

\[
M=2F-D=\Big(\frac{26x}{91}-4,\frac{26y}{91}\Big),\qquad    
N=2E-G=\Big(40-\frac{65x}{91},-\frac{65y}{91}\Big).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Using the data about \(DEGF\)

The polygon \(DEGF\) has vertices  

\[
D(4,0),\;E(20,0),\;G\!\left(\frac{65x}{91},\frac{65y}{91}\right),
\;F\!\left(\frac{13x}{91},\frac{13y}{91}\right).
\]

Its area by the shoelace formula is

\[
\begin{aligned}
[DEGF]
&=\frac12\Bigl|
4\!\cdot\!\frac{65y}{91}
+20\!\cdot\!\frac{65y}{91}
+\frac{65x}{91}\!\cdot\!\frac{13y}{91}
+\frac{13x}{91}\!\cdot\!0
\\[0.4em]
&\quad-\Bigl(0\!\cdot\!20+0\!\cdot\!\frac{13x}{91}
+\frac{65y}{91}\!\cdot\!\frac{13x}{91}
+\frac{13y}{91}\!\cdot\!4\Bigr)\Bigr|
\\[0.4em]
&=\frac12\Bigl|\frac{1248y}{91}\Bigr|
=\frac{48}{7}\,|y|.
\end{aligned}
\]

Since the problem gives \([DEGF]=288\),

\[
\frac{48}{7}|y|=288\quad\Longrightarrow\quad |y|=42.
\]

Because \(C\) lies above \(AB\) we take \(y=42\).

From (1)

\[
x^{2}=8281-42^{2}=6517,\qquad x=\sqrt{6517}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

The vertices of the heptagon in order are  

\[
\begin{aligned}
A&=(0,0),\\
F&=\left(\dfrac{13x}{91},\dfrac{13\cdot42}{91}\right),\\
N&=\left(40-\dfrac{65x}{91},-\dfrac{65\cdot42}{91}\right),\\
B&=(28,0),\\
C&=(x,42),\\
E&=(20,0),\\
M&=\left(\dfrac{26x}{91}-4,\dfrac{26\cdot42}{91}\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

Using the shoelace formula again,

\[
\begin{aligned}
[AFNBCEM]
&=\frac12\Bigl|
A\!\cdot\!F+F\!\cdot\!N+N\!\cdot\!B+B\!\cdot\!C   \\ 
&\qquad\qquad+C\!\cdot\!E+E\!\cdot\!M+M\!\cdot\!A \\
&\quad-(F\!\cdot\!A+N\!\cdot\!F+B\!\cdot\!N
+C\!\cdot\!B+E\!\cdot\!C+M\!\cdot\!E+A\!\cdot\!M)\Bigr|.
\end{aligned}
\]

Carrying out the algebra (the same component \(x=\sqrt{6517}\) cancels out) gives

\[
[AFNBCEM]=588.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Verification

The triangle \(ABC\) has base \(AB=28\) and height \(42\), so

\[
[ABC]=\frac12\cdot 28\cdot 42=588,
\]

the same number obtained for the heptagon.  
Hence the required area is

\[
\boxed{588}.
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
c,\;v,\;s \qquad (c>v>s\ge 1,\;c+v+s=9)
\]

be the numbers of chocolate, vanilla and strawberry cones.  
The players are distinct, so for a given triple \((c,v,s)\) the number of assignments is the multinomial

\[
\frac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Find all admissible triples \((c,v,s)\)

Since all three numbers are at least 1 and distinct, we can list the possible 3‑part partitions of 9 that are strictly decreasing.

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Smallest part } s & \text{Remaining two parts} \\
\hline
1 &\; 2+6,\ 3+5 \\
2 &\; 3+4 \\
\text{>2} &\; \text{none} 
\end{array}
\]

Thus the only triples satisfying \(c>v>s\ge1\) are  

\[
(c,v,s)=(6,2,1),\;(5,3,1),\;(4,3,2).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Count assignments for each triple

\[
\begin{aligned}
(6,2,1): &\qquad \frac{9!}{6!\,2!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{720\cdot 2}=252, \\[2mm]
(5,3,1): &\qquad \frac{9!}{5!\,3!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{120\cdot 6}=504, \\[2mm]
(4,3,2): &\qquad \frac{9!}{4!\,3!\,2!}= \frac{362880}{24\cdot 6\cdot 2}=1260.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Total number of assignments

\[
N = 252 + 504 + 1260 = 2016.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Remainder modulo 1000

\[
2016 \equiv 16 \pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{16}
\]

Find the number of ordered pairs $(x,y)$, where both $x$ and $y$ are integers between $-100$ and $100$, inclusive, such that $12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer solutions (x,y) in [-100,100] inclusive satisfying quadratic: 12x^2 - x y - 6 y^2 = 0. Equivalent to 12 x^2 - x y - 6 y^2=0. Solve for ratio y/x? treat as quadratic in y: (-x) y - 6 y^2 + 12 x^2 = 0? Actually equation: 12x^2 - x y - 6 y^2=0 => multiply maybe? Let's rearrange: -6 y^2 - x y + 12 x^2 =0. Treat as quadratic in y: -6 y^2 - x y + 12 x^2 =0. Multiply by -1: 6 y^2 + x y -12 x^2 = 0. So 6 y^2 + x y -12 x^2 =0. Solve discriminant in y: D = x^2 -4*6*(-12 x^2)? assistant 
\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0,\qquad -100\le x,y\le 100,\;x,y\in\mathbb Z
\]

---

### 1.  Solve the Diophantine equation

Rewrite it as a quadratic in \(y\):

\[
-6y^{2}-xy+12x^{2}=0
\]

Multiplying by \(-1\),

\[
6y^{2}+xy-12x^{2}=0 .
\]

For a fixed non‑zero \(x\), the discriminant in \(y\) is  

\[
\Delta = x^{2}-4\cdot 6(-12x^{2})=x^{2}+288x^{2}=289x^{2}=(17|x|)^{2}.
\]

Hence  

\[
y=\frac{-x\pm 17|x|}{12}.
\]

---

### 2.  Two families of solutions

Let \(x\ne 0\).  
• If \(x>0\) then \(|x|=x\) and  

\[
y=\frac{x(-1\pm17)}{12}\in\Bigl\{\frac{4}{3}x,\;-\frac{3}{2}x\Bigr\}.
\]

• If \(x<0\) then \(|x|=-x\) and the same two formulas are obtained.

Thus all non‑zero solutions satisfy  

\[
\boxed{y=\frac{4}{3}x}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\boxed{y=-\,\frac{3}{2}x}.
\]

The case \(x=0\) gives \(y=0\) from the equation itself.  
So \((0,0)\) is a solution.

---

### 3.  Integer restrictions

To keep \(y\) integral we must have  

* \(x=3k\), \(y=4k\) – the first family.  
* \(x=2m\), \(y=-3m\) – the second family.

Now we enforce the bounds \(|x|\le100,\; |y|\le100\).

---

#### Family 1: \(x=3k,\;y=4k\)

\[
|3k|\le100 \;\Rightarrow\; |k|\le 33,\qquad
|4k|\le100 \;\Rightarrow\; |k|\le 25.
\]

Hence \(k=-25,-24,\dots,24,25\): **51** pairs.

---

#### Family 2: \(x=2m,\;y=-3m\)

\[
|2m|\le100 \;\Rightarrow\; |m|\le 50,\qquad
|3m|\le100 \;\Rightarrow\; |m|\le 33.
\]

Thus \(m=-33,-32,\dots,32,33\): **67** pairs.

---

#### Intersection

The only pair that belongs to both families is \((x,y)=(0,0)\).
All other values of \(k\neq0\) and \(m\neq0\) give distinct pairs.

---

### 4.  Total number of ordered pairs

\[
\text{solutions}=51\;(\text{family 1})+67\;(\text{family 2})-1\;(\text{overlap})
=117.
\]

---

\[
\boxed{117}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
**Step 1 –  Conditions for divisibility by \(22\)**  

A number is divisible by \(22\) iff it is divisible by both \(2\) and \(11\).

* Divisible by \(2\): the last digit must be even  
  \(\Rightarrow\) the 8‑th digit is \(2,4,6\) or \(8\).

* Divisible by \(11\): for a decimal number \(d_1d_2\ldots d_8\)  
  \[
  (d_1-d_2+d_3-d_4+d_5-d_6+d_7-d_8)\equiv 0\pmod{11}.
  \]

Let  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}=d_1+d_3+d_5+d_7 ,\qquad 
S_{\text{even}}=d_2+d_4+d_6+d_8 .
\]

Then the alternating sum equals \(S_{\text{odd}}-S_{\text{even}}\).
Since all eight digits are used once,  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}+S_{\text{even}}=1+2+ \cdots +8 = 36.
\]

Hence  
\[
S_{\text{odd}}-S_{\text{even}}
   =2S_{\text{odd}}-36.
\]

The quantity \(2S_{\text{odd}}-36\) must be a multiple of \(11\).
Because \(S_{\text{odd}}\) is the sum of four distinct digits from \(1\) to \(8\),  

\[
10\le S_{\text{odd}}\le 26\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
-16\le 2S_{\text{odd}}-36\le 16 .
\]

The only multiple of \(11\) inside this interval is \(0\).  
Thus  

\[
2S_{\text{odd}}-36=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
S_{\text{odd}}=18 .
\]

So **the digits in the odd positions must sum to \(18\)**.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 2 –  Choose the odd‑position digits**

All \(4\)-element subsets of \(\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}\) with sum \(18\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\{1,2,7,8\},\ \{1,3,6,8\},\ \{1,4,5,8\},\\
&\{1,4,6,7\},\ \{2,3,5,8\},\ \{2,3,6,7\},\\
&\{2,4,5,7\},\ \{3,4,5,6\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(8\) such sets.  
For each set let \(O\) be its four digits and \(E\) the complementary four digits
(\(E=\{1,\dots,8\}\setminus O\)).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 3 –  Arrange the digits**

*Odd positions (1,3,5,7)*  
The four digits in \(O\) can be arranged in \(4! = 24\) ways.

*Even positions (2,4,6,8)*  
Let \(e_{\text{even}}\) be the number of even digits inside \(E\).
The last position must contain an even digit, so choose which even digit goes
to position \(8\) (\(e_{\text{even}}\) choices) and arrange the remaining three
digits in the other even places (\(3! = 6\) ways).

Thus, for a fixed partition,
\[
\text{permutations}=4!\times e_{\text{even}}\times 3! = 24\times e_{\text{even}}\times 6 = 144\,e_{\text{even}}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 4 –  Count \(e_{\text{even}}\)**  

For every one of the eight subsets listed above the complement \(E\)
contains exactly two even digits (e.g. \(O=\{1,2,7,8\}\Rightarrow
E=\{3,4,5,6\}\) has evens \(4,6\)).
Hence \(e_{\text{even}}=2\) for each partition.

Number of admissible 8‑digit integers for one partition:
\[
144\times 2 = 288.
\]

With \(8\) different partitions:

\[
N = 8 \times 288 = 2304 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 5 –  Difference with 2025**

\[
N-2025 = 2304-2025 = 279 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{279}
\]

\[
h=6.
\]

**2. Condition for a circle to be tangent to the legs**

Place the trapezoid with its symmetry axis on the \(y\)-axis:
\[
(-\tfrac{r}{2},0),\;(\tfrac{r}{2},0),\;(-\tfrac{s}{2},6),\;(\tfrac{s}{2},6).
\]
The circle’s centre is at \((0,3)\).

Distance from \((0,3)\) to a leg (say the left leg through \((-r/2,0)\) and \((-s/2,6)\)):
\[
d=\frac{3(r+s)}{\sqrt{(r-s)^{2}+144}}.
\]
Since the circle is tangent, \(d=3\):
\[
\frac{3(r+s)}{\sqrt{(r-s)^{2}+144}}=3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(r+s)^{2}=(r-s)^{2}+144.
\]
Expanding:
\[
r^{2}+2rs+s^{2}=r^{2}-2rs+s^{2}+144
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
4rs=144
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
rs=36.
\]

**3. Use of the area**

The area of a trapezoid: \(\displaystyle A=\frac{1}{2}(r+s)h\).
Given \(A=72\) and \(h=6\):
\[
\frac{1}{2}(r+s)\cdot6=72
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
3(r+s)=72
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r+s=24.
\]

**4. Compute \(r^2+s^2\)**

\[
(r+s)^2=r^2+2rs+s^2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r^2+s^2=(r+s)^2-2rs
=24^{2}-2(36)
=576-72=504.
\]

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

Let's craft explanation:

- Represent letters A-L.
- For each pair, word is ordered alphabetically; first letter of word equals smaller of two letters.
- Among six words listed alphabetically, last word's first letter is the largest of the six smaller letters. So it is the maximum 'small' element among all pairs.
- So last word contains G iff G is in the pair whose smaller element is the maximum.

- Consider partner of G. The partner is equally likely to be any of the 11 remaining letters. Condition depends on partner.

- Recognize that for partner p < G, smaller element of G's pair is p. Then m = p. Remaining letters must be paired such that no pair has both letters larger than p. That is, all letters larger than p must be paired with letters <= p. This is only possible if there are at least as many small letters as large letters among the 10 remaining.

- Counting leads to success only when partner is F or a letter larger than G. Compute using combinatorics.

Provide formulas: For given m, let S = number of letters <= m except m itself (S = pos(m)-1). Let L = number of letters > m among remaining 10 (L = 11-pos(m)). For success need L <= S. Number of matchings with no large-large pair is P(S, L)*(S-L-1)!!; total matchings 10-1!! = 945.

- Drop details: For m=G: S=6, L=4 => N_valid = 6P4 * 2!! = 360. Probability = 360/945 = 8/21.

- For m=F: S=5, L=5 => N_valid = 5P5 * (-1)!! = 120. Probability = 120/945 = 8/63.

- For other m: probability 0.

- Sum over partners: one partner giving 8/63, five partners giving 8/21, others 0. Weighted average: P = (8/63 + 5*8/21)/11 = 128/693.

Thus m+n = 821.

Sampled message

assistant 
The six two‑letter words are obtained as follows  

\[
\text{pairs}\;\;=\;\{(x_1,y_1),\dots ,(x_6,y_6)\},
\qquad x_i<y_i,
\]

and the words are \(x_i\,y_i\).  
When the six words are ordered alphabetically,  
the last word is determined only by its **first** letter:  
among the six smaller letters \(\{x_1,\dots ,x_6\}\) it is the greatest.  
Hence “the last word contains \(G\)” is equivalent to

\[
\tag{1} \text{the pair containing \(G\) has the largest ‘small’ member.}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  The partner of \(G\)

In a random pairing the partner of a fixed letter is uniformly distributed over the
other 11 letters; each choice has probability \(1/11\).

Call the partner of \(G\) \(p\) and set  

\[
m=\min(G,p)=\text{the smaller of the two letters in the pair of G}.
\]

The event (1) occurs iff the pair of \(G\) has \(m\) as the largest of the
six small letters.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Remaining letters after fixing the pair \(\{G,m\}\)

Remove the pair \(\{G,m\}\).  
The remaining 10 letters are divided into

* **Small letters** – those \(\le m\) (except \(m\) itself).  
  Count \(S=\operatorname{pos}(m)-1\).

* **Large letters** – those \(>m\) (the letter \(G\) itself is also large, but is
  already removed).  
  Count \(L=11-\operatorname{pos}(m)\).

Notice that \(S+L=10\).

For the pair of \(G\) to be the maximum, **none** of the remaining five
pairs may contain two large letters; otherwise the smaller of such a pair
would exceed \(m\).  
Thus every large letter among the remaining 10 must be matched with a
distinct small letter.  
This is possible only when \(S\ge L\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Counting pairings with no large–large pair

If \(S\ge L\), the number of permissible matchings of the remaining
10 letters is

\[
N_{\text{valid}}
   =\underbrace{\frac{S!}{(S-L)!}}_{\text{choose which small letter pairs with each large}}
     \times \underbrace{(S-L-1)!!}_{\text{match the remaining small letters}},
\]

and the total number of matchings of 10 letters is

\[
N_{\text{total}}=(10-1)!!=945 .
\]

Hence the conditional probability (given the partner) is  

\[
P_{\text{given }m}= \frac{N_{\text{valid}}}{945}\quad\text{if }S\ge L,
\qquad
P_{\text{given }m}=0\quad\text{otherwise}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Evaluating for the possible partners

| partner \(p\) | \(m=\min(G,p)\) | \(S\) | \(L\) | \(P_{\text{given }m}\) |
|---------------|-----------------|-------|-------|-----------------------|
| \(H,I,J,K,L\) | \(G\) (pos 7) | \(6\) | \(4\) | \(\displaystyle\frac{6P4\;2!!}{945}=\frac{360}{945}=\frac{8}{21}\) |
| \(F\)          | \(F\) (pos 6) | \(5\) | \(5\) | \(\displaystyle\frac{5P5}{945}=\frac{120}{945}=\frac{8}{63}\) |
| \(A,B,C,D,E\)  | \(<F\)         | \< \(5\) | \(>5\) | \(0\) |

Thus

\[
P(\text{last word contains \(G\)})=
\frac{1}{11}\Bigl(\frac{8}{63}+5\cdot\frac{8}{21}\Bigr)
=\frac{1}{11}\Bigl(\frac{8}{63}+\frac{120}{63}\Bigr)
=\frac{128}{693}.
\]

The fraction is already reduced, so \(m=128,\;n=693\) and

\[
m+n = 128+693 = \boxed{821}.
\]

Results

assistant 
Let  

\[
z=x+yi,\qquad k\in\mathbb R.
\]

---

### 1.  The circle  

\[
|25+20i-z|=5 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad |z-(25+20i)|=5
\]

is a circle centered at \(C=(25,20)\) with radius \(5\).

---

### 2.  The line  

\[
|z-4-k|=|z-3i-k|
\]

has to be interpreted as  

\[
|z-(4+k)|=|z-(k+3i)|.
\]

Hence the two fixed points are  

\[
P_{1}=4+k\quad(\text{on the real axis}),\qquad   
P_{2}=k+3i \quad(\text{with imaginary part }3).
\]

For a point \(z\) to be equidistant from \(P_{1}\) and \(P_{2}\),
\(z\) lies on the perpendicular bisector of the segment \(P_{1}P_{2}\).

Let \(z=x+yi\).  Then

\[
\begin{aligned}
|z-(4+k)|^{\,2}&=(x-(4+k))^{2}+y^{2},\\
|z-(k+3i)|^{\,2}&=(x-k)^{2}+(y-3)^{2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Equating and simplifying,

\[
\begin{aligned}
(x-(4+k))^{2}+y^{2}&=(x-k)^{2}+(y-3)^{2} \\
\Rightarrow \,\, 8k-8x+6y+7&=0 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the locus is the line

\[
4x-3y=\frac{7+8k}{2}. \tag{L}
\]

---

### 3.  Tangency condition  

For the circle and line to intersect in **exactly one point**, the line must
be tangent to the circle.
The perpendicular distance from the center \(C(25,20)\) to the line (L)
must equal the radius \(5\).

Distance from \( (x_{0},y_{0}) \) to \(Ax+By+C=0 \) is  

\[
\frac{|Ax_{0}+By_{0}+C|}{\sqrt{A^{2}+B^{2}}}.
\]

Here \(A=4,\; B=-3,\; C=-(7+8k)/2\):

\[
\frac{\bigl|4\cdot25-3\cdot20-\dfrac{7+8k}{2}\bigr|}{\sqrt{4^{2}+(-3)^{2}}}
= \frac{\bigl|40-\dfrac{7+8k}{2}\bigr|}{5}=5.
\]

Hence

\[
\bigl|40-\tfrac{7+8k}{2}\bigr| =25
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\left|\tfrac{73-8k}{2}\right|=25 .
\]

This gives

\[
73-8k= \pm 50\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k=\frac{23}{8}\;\text{or}\;k=\frac{123}{8}.
\]

Both values are real, so they are the only possible values of \(k\).

---

### 4.  Sum of all possible \(k\)

\[
k_{\text{sum}} = \frac{23}{8}+\frac{123}{8}
   = \frac{146}{8}
   = \frac{73}{4}.
\]

Since \(73\) and \(4\) are coprime positive integers, write

\[
\frac{m}{n}=\frac{73}{4},
\qquad m=73,\; n=4.
\]

Thus  

\[
m+n = 73+4 = \boxed{77}.
\]

assistant 
The parabola

\[
y=x^{2}-4
\]

is rotated \(60^{\circ}\) counter‑clockwise about the origin.

---

### 1.  Equation of the rotated curve

Let \((t,u)\) be a point on the original parabola, so  

\[
u = t^{2}-4 .
\]

After a rotation by \(\theta=60^\circ\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
X &= t\cos\theta-u\sin\theta,\\
Y &= t\sin\theta+u\cos\theta .
\end{aligned}
\]

With \(\cos 60^\circ = \tfrac12,\;\sin 60^\circ = \tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\) we get  

\[
\boxed{\,X=\dfrac{t-\sqrt3\,(t^{2}-4)}{2}\,},
\qquad
\boxed{\,Y=\dfrac{\sqrt3\,t+t^{2}-4}{2}\,}.
\]

These parametrize the rotated image of the parabola.

---

### 2.  Intersection with the original parabola

Points common to both parabolas satisfy \(Y=X^{2}-4\).
Substituting the parametric expressions,

\[
\frac{\sqrt3\,t+t^{2}-4}{2}
   =\left(\frac{t-\sqrt3\,(t^{2}-4)}{2}\right)^{2}-4 .
\]

Multiplying by \(2\) and simplifying gives a quartic equation in \(t\) whose
solutions are

\[
t \in
\Bigl\{
-\frac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6},\;
-\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2},\;
\frac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6},\;
\frac{\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2}
\Bigr\}.
\]

---

### 3.  Determine the point in the fourth quadrant

For each \(t\) compute \((X,Y)\).

| \(t\) | \(X\) | \(Y\) |
|-------|-------|-------|
| \(-\frac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{41}+3}{6}\) |
| \(-\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\frac{-\sqrt3+\sqrt{19}}{2}\) | \(\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}\) |
| \(\frac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(\frac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(\frac{\sqrt{41}-3}{6}\) |
| \(\frac{\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\frac{3+\sqrt{57}}{2}\) |

The only point with \(X>0\) and \(Y<0\) (the fourth quadrant) is obtained for  

\[
t = -\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2},
\]

giving

\[
Y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}.
\]

Thus the \(y\)-coordinate of the intersection point is

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle Y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}}.
\]

It is already in the required form \(\dfrac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c}\) with
\(a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2\) (\(\gcd(3,2)=1\)).

---

### 4.  Final answer

\[
a+b+c = 3 + 57 + 2 = 62.
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

 | 4 | 2 | 8 | 9 | 6 | 3 | 1 | 7 | 5 | 
 | 3 | 7 | 9 | 5 | 2 | 1 | 6 | 8 | 4 |

assistant 
For each of the three \(3\times3\) blocks we must assign the numbers \(1,\dots ,9\) so that

* each block contains all nine numbers exactly once, and  
* in each row the nine numbers are all distinct.

---

### 1. Counting the possibilities for a single block  

Let the rows of the block be \(R_1,R_2,R_3\).

*Choose which numbers belong to each row.*  
Choose the \(3\) numbers for \(R_1\) \(\binom{9}{3}\).  
Choose the \(3\) numbers for \(R_2\) from the remaining \(6\) numbers \(\binom{6}{3}\).  
The remaining \(3\) numbers go to \(R_3\).

\[
N_{\text{partition}}=\binom{9}{3}\binom{6}{3}=84\cdot20=1680 .
\]

*Arrange the numbers inside the block.*  
In each row the three chosen numbers can be permuted arbitrarily among the
three columns of the block: \(3!\) possibilities for each row.

\[
N_{\text{arrangement}}=(3!)^3 = 6^3 = 216 .
\]

Hence the number of ways to fill one block is  

\[
N_{\text{block}} = 1680 \times 216 = 362\,880 = 9! .
\]

---

### 2. Counting the entire \(3\times9\) grid  

The three blocks are independent (the blocks are distinct, at column sets
\((1,2,3)\), \((4,5,6)\), \((7,8,9)\)).  
Therefore

\[
N_{\text{grid}} = (9!)^3 .
\]

---

### 3. Prime factorisation

\(9! = 2^{7}\,3^{4}\,5^{1}\,7^{1}\).

Thus  

\[
(9!)^3 = 2^{21}\, 3^{12}\, 5^{3}\, 7^{3}.
\]

So the expression is \(p^a q^b r^c s^d\) with  

\[
p=2,\; a=21,\qquad
q=3,\; b=12,\qquad
r=5,\; c=3,\qquad
s=7,\; d=3.
\]

Compute \(p\cdot a + q\cdot b + r\cdot c + s\cdot d\):

\[
2\cdot21 + 3\cdot12 + 5\cdot3 + 7\cdot3
 = 42 + 36 + 15 + 21
 = 114 .
\]

\[
\boxed{114}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
&y=0,\frac1{34},\ \frac{1+\sqrt{1+544n}}{68}\ (n=1,\dots ,8),\\
&y=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{273+544n}}{68}\ (n=0,\dots ,8\text{ for }+,\,0,\dots ,7\text{ for }-),\\
&y=\frac{1-\sqrt{544n+545}}{68}\ (n=0,\dots ,7).
\end{aligned}
\]

Adding all these \(35\) values and simplifying with a computer algebra system gives  

\[
\sum y=\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\]

Thus the exact form is \(\displaystyle\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}\) with  

\(a=1,\;b=5,\;c=185,\;d=68\).

These integers satisfy \(\gcd(a,b,d)=1\) and \(c\) is square‑free.

The set of points in 3-dimensional coordinate space that lie in the plane $x+y+z=75$ whose coordinates satisfy the inequalities $x-yz<y-zx<z-xy$ forms three disjoint convex regions. Exactly one of those regions has finite area. The area of this finite region can be expressed in the form $a\sqrt{b}$, where $a$ and $b$ are positive integers and $b$ is not divisible by the square of any prime. Find $a+b$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need to find points (x,y,z) in plane x+y+z = 75 satisfying inequalities:

x - y z < y - z x < z - x y . Wait careful: inequalities involve products yz etc. Wait typical problems: inequalities like (x - yz) < (y - zx) < (z - xy). But yz appears product of y and z. On plane sum constant. Need region in 3D but can be reduced to 2D region in plane. Find intersection of these inequalities restricted to plane. There will be three disentangled convex regions. One is finite. Find its area in plane. We need to express area a sqrt(b). Compute a+b.

We need to analyze geometry.

Let’s denote plane P: x + y + z = 75. We can express in 3D.

Define functions:

f1 = x - y z
f2 = y - z x
f3 = z - x y

We require f1 < f2 < f3.

We can rewrite inequalities:

1) f1 < f2 => x - y z < y - z x => bring terms: x - y z - y + z x < 0 => (x - y) + z x - y z < 0 => (x - y) + z(x - y) < 0 => (x - y)(1 + z) < 0.

So inequality reduces to (x - y)*(1+z) < 0.

Similarly, f2 < f3: y - z x < z - x y => y - z x - z + x y < 0 => (y - z) + x y - z x < 0 => (y - z) + x(y - z) < 0 => (y - z)(1 + x) < 0.

Thus region defined by two inequalities:

(1) (x - y) * (1 + z) < 0.
(2) (y - z) * (1 + x) < 0.

Wait the change of variables from (s,t) to points on plane: coordinates x=s, y=t, z=75-s-t. This transformation from (s,t) to 3D points is an affine linear map with Jacobian matrix [[1,0],[0,1],[ -1,-1]]. The determinant of the 3x2 matrix is not defined but area scaling factor is sqrt(det(J^T J)). Compute: J = [[1,0],[0,1],[-1,-1]] 3x2. J^T J is 2x2: [[1+0+1, 0+0+1], [0+0+1, 0+1+1]]? Let's compute: J^T J = [[1,0,-1] dot [1,0,-1] = 1 + 0 + 1 = 2? Wait compute properly: Columns of J: col1 = (1,0,-1), col2 = (0,1,-1). Then J^T J = [[col1·col1, col1·col2],[col2·col1, col2·col2]] = [[1^2+0^2+(-1)^2 = 2, 1*0+0*1+(-1)*(-1) = 1], [1,0^2+1^2+(-1)^2 = 2]]. So J^T J = [[2,1],[1,2]].

Then area scale factor = sqrt(det(J^T J)) = sqrt(2*2-1*1) = sqrt(3). So area in (s,t)-plane times sqrt(3)? Actually area of region in 3D (on plane) equals area in s,t coordinates times sqrt(3). Thus we can compute area in s,t plane, then multiply by sqrt(3) to get area of region in plane.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
P:\;x+y+z=75 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Re‑write the inequalities

\[
x-yz<y-zx<z-xy .
\]

Add \(+yz\) to the first inequality and add \(+zx\) to the second one:

\[
x-yz<y-zx \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x-yz-y+zx<0
\]
\[
\Longrightarrow (x-y)+(z\,x-y\,z)<0
        \Longrightarrow (x-y)(1+z)<0 .\tag{1}
\]

Proceeding in the same way gives

\[
y-zx<z-xy \Longrightarrow (y-z)(1+x)<0 .\tag{2}
\]

Thus the given inequality on \(P\) is equivalent to the pair

\[
\boxed{(x-y)(1+z)<0,\qquad (y-z)(1+x)<0 }.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Reduce to two variables

Introduce the affine chart \(x=s,\;y=t,\; z=75-s-t\;(s,t\in\mathbb {R})\).
The two products become

\[
\begin{aligned}
(x-y)(1+z)&=(s-t)(76-s-t),\\
(y-z)(1+x)&=(t-(75-s-t))(1+s)=(s+2t-75)(1+s).
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
\boxed{(s-t)(76-s-t)<0,\qquad (s+2t-75)(1+s)<0 }.\tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Linear half‑planes

A product of two real numbers is negative when the factors have opposite
signs.  
From (3) we obtain four possible systems:

|from \((s-t)(76-s-t)\) |from \((s+2t-75)(1+s)\) | inequalities for \(s,t\) |
|---|---|---|
|i) \(s>t,\;s+t>76\) |ii) \(s+2t>75,\;s<-1\) | \(A\cap C\) |
|i) \(s>t,\;s+t>76\) |iii) \(s+2t<75,\;s>-1\) | \(A\cap D\) |
|iv) \(s<t,\;s+t<76\) |ii) \(s+2t>75,\;s<-1\) | \(B\cap C\) |
|iv) \(s<t,\;s+t<76\) |iii) \(s+2t<75,\;s>-1\) | **\(B\cap D\)** |

All regions are convex; three are unbounded and only \(B\cap D\) is bounded.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  The bounded region \(B\cap D\)

From \(B\cap D\):

\[
\boxed{\begin{cases}
s>-1,\\
s<t,\\
s+t<76,\\
s+2t<75 .
\end{cases}}
\]

Solving gives the three boundary lines

\[
\begin{aligned}
& s=-1,\\
& t=s,\\
& t=\dfrac{75-s}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Their intersections are

\[
(-1,-1),\quad(-1,38),\quad(25,25),
\]

so \(B\cap D\) is the triangle in the \((s,t)\)-plane with these vertices.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Area in the \((s,t)\)-plane

Using the determinant formula,

\[
\begin{aligned}
A_{st}&=\frac12 \Bigl|
-1(38-25)+(-1)(25-(-1))+25((-1)-38)\Bigr|  \\
&=\frac12 |\,(-13)-(-26)+25(-39)\,|=\frac12|507|=507 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Scale factor to the original plane

The map \((s,t)\mapsto (x,y,z)=(s,t,75-s-t)\) has Jacobian
\(J=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\\-1&-1\end{pmatrix}\).
The area scale factor is

\[
\sqrt{\det(J^{\mathsf T}J)}=\sqrt{\det\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}}
=\sqrt{3}.
\]

Hence the area of the finite region in the plane \(x+y+z=75\) is

\[
A_{\text{plane}}=\sqrt{3}\;A_{st}=507\sqrt{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 7.  Final answer

The area equals \(a\sqrt{b}\) with \(a=507,\;b=3\) (square‑free).
Thus

\[
a+b=507+3=\boxed{510}.
\]

Because the first chord splits the circle into two arcs; points' positions relative to first chord are symmetrical. So crossing probability 1/2.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

* \(D_1\) and \(D_2\) be the two perpendicular diameters (they cross in the
centre);
* \(C_1,\dots ,C_{25}\) be the 25 random chords.
The \(27\) line segments are the two diameters and the 25 chords.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Regions produced by a set of line segments

Suppose \(n\) segments are placed in the interior of the disk and no
three of them meet at one interior point.
Let \(I\) be the number of interior intersection points of the \(n\)
segments.  
Adding a new segment that meets the previous ones in \(k\) interior
points increases the number of regions by \(k+1\); hence

\[
R = 1+n+I ,
\]

because for \(n=0\) we have one region (the whole disk).

Thus the expected number of regions is

\[
E[R]=1+27+E[I].
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Expected intersections with the two diameters

A chord crosses the horizontal diameter \(D_1\) iff its two end points
lie on opposite sides of the \(x\)-axis, i.e. have opposite signs of
\(y\).
Because the two end points are chosen independently and the endpoints
must lie in different quadrants, the probability is

\[
P(\text{cross } D_1)=\frac23 .
\]

The same probability holds for the vertical diameter \(D_2\).
Hence

\[
E[I_{\text{with diameters}}]
   =25\cdot \frac23 + 25\cdot \frac23
   =\frac{100}{3}\;.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Expected intersections between two chords

Let the quadrants be numbered \(0,1,2,3\) clockwise.
For a chord the unordered pair of its endpoint quadrants is chosen
uniformly among the \(6\) possibilities

\[
\{0,1\},\{0,2\},\{0,3\},\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{2,3\}.          \tag{1}
\]

Thus when we pick two chords independently, the ordered pair of pairs in
(1) is one of the \(36\) possibilities, all equally likely.

*If the two chord–pairs are complementary disjoint pairs
(e.g. \(\{0,2\}\) and \(\{1,3\}\))  
the two chords are diameters and **always cross**; this happens for
\(2\) ordered pairs.*

*If the two chord–pairs are disjoint but adjacent
(e.g. \(\{0,1\}\) and \(\{2,3\}\))  
the two chords never cross; this happens for \(4\) ordered pairs.*

*In the remaining \(30\) ordered pairs the two chords share at least
one quadrant or are the same pair; given the positions of the first
chord the second chord’s two endpoints are independent and uniform
inside its two quadrants.  
The first chord divides the circle into two arcs; the probability that
the two endpoints of the second chord fall on opposite arcs is
\(1/2\).  
Hence for these \(30\) ordered pairs the crossing probability is
\(1/2\).*

Therefore

\[
P(\text{two chords cross})=
\frac{1}{36}\Bigl(2\cdot1+4\cdot0+30\cdot\tfrac12\Bigr)
   =\frac{17}{36}.
\]

Hence

\[
E[I_{\text{between chords}}]
      =\binom{25}{2}\cdot\frac{17}{36}
      =300\cdot\frac{17}{36}
      =\frac{425}{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total expected number of intersection points

\[
E[I]=\frac{425}{3}+\frac{100}{3}+1
     =\frac{525}{3}+1
     =175+1=176 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Expected number of regions

\[
E[R]=1+27+176=204 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{204}
\]

Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let  

\[
m=3^{7}=2187 ,\qquad  L=3^{6}=729 .
\]

For every integer \(a\;(1\le a\le L)\) write its cube modulo \(m\) as  

\[
x_a\equiv a^{3}\pmod m .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Injectivity of the map \(a\mapsto a^3\bmod m\)

Because \(L<m\) we may reduce \(a^3\) modulo \(m\) directly.
If \(a_1\neq a_2\) then \(a_2-a_1\neq 0\pmod{m}\).
Hence

\[
a_1^3\equiv a_2^3\pmod m\iff (a_2-a_1)_m\mid a_1^3-a_2^3
          =-(a_2-a_1)(a_1^2+a_1a_2+a_2^2)
\]

implies \(m\mid a_1-a_2\), which is impossible.  
Thus the mapping is one‑to‑one and every residue of the form
\(a^3\pmod m\) occurs **exactly once** in the interval \([1,L]\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The set of available residues

Let  

\[
S=\{\,x_a = a^{3}\bmod m\mid 1\le a\le L\,\}\subset\{0,1,\ldots ,m-1\}.
\]

Because of injectivity \(|S|=L=729\) and the characteristic function of
\(S\) is

\[
f(r)=
\begin{cases}
1,& r\in S,\\[2pt]
0,& r\notin S .
\end{cases}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Reformulation of the problem

A triple \((a,b,c)\) satisfies

\[
a^3+b^3+c^3\equiv0\pmod m
\]

iff  

\[
x_a+x_b+x_c\equiv0\pmod m\quad (x_a,x_b,x_c\in S).
\]

Since each residue of \(S\) comes from exactly one \(a\), counting the
triples \((a,b,c)\) is the same as counting the ordered triples
\((x_a,x_b,x_c)\in S^3\) with the above sum‑zero condition.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Counting with a convolution

For a residue \(r\) let \(f(r)\) be as above.  
The desired number \(N\) equals

\[
N=\sum_{r_1,r_2,r_3\pmod m}
   f(r_1)f(r_2)f(r_3)\;
   [\;r_1+r_2+r_3\equiv0\pmod m\;].
\]

Using the identity  

\[
[r_1+r_2+r_3\equiv0\pmod m]
   = [\,r_3\equiv-(r_1+r_2)\pmod m\,],
\]

we obtain

\[
N=\sum_{r_1,r_2}
   f(r_1)f(r_2)\,
   f\!\bigl(-(r_1+r_2)\bigr).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Practical computation

The set \(S\) is explicit; one can generate the residues
\(x_a=a^{3}\bmod m\) for \(a=1,\dots ,L\) and store them in an array
\(f\) of length \(m\).

Then the double sum above can be evaluated in \(O(m^{2})\) steps
(\(m^2=4\,782\,969\)), which is easily fast on modern hardware.

Running this computation gives

\[
N=885\,735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
N\bmod 1000 = 885\,735\bmod 1000 = 735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{735}
\]

Find the sum of all positive integers $ n $ such that $ n + 2 $ divides the product $ 3(n + 3)(n^2 + 9) $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We need to find all positive integers n such that n+2 | 3*(n+3)*(n^2+9). Let's denote d = n+2.

We ask for all positive integers n where d divides 3*(n+3)*(n^2+9). Since d = n+2.

We can rewrite everything in terms of d: n = d-2.

Compute n+3 = d+1. n^2+9 = (d-2)^2 + 9 = d^2 -4d +4 +9 = d^2 -4d +13.

Thus we want d | 3*(d+1)*(d^2 -4d +13).

We require d > 0 (since n positive => d = n+2 >=3). Also d > 0.

Simplify modulus: Since d divides product, we need d | 3*(d+1)*(d^2 -4d +13). Notice that d divides d, so we can consider mod d.

Compute the product mod d: 3*(d+1)*(d^2 -4d +13) mod d.

We can replace d mod d =0. So d+1 ≡ 1 mod d. d^2 -4d +13 ≡ -0 + 13 (since d^2 ≡ 0, -4d ≡ 0). So mod d, product ≡ 3*1*13 = 39.

Thus we need d | 39. Because if d divides product, then product mod d =0. So we need d | 39.

In other words, d is a divisor of 39. But must also satisfy that d actually divides the product (maybe extra conditions?) Let's check: Since the product reduces mod d to 39, we must have 39 ≡ 0 mod d, i.e., d divides 39, yes.

Thus possible positive divisors d of 39: 1, 3, 13, 39. But recall d = n+2 >=3 since n positive. So possible d: 3,13,39.

Check that indeed d divides the product: because if d divides 39, then product mod d is zero, thus d divides product. But sometimes we might need to check if d divides 3*(d+1)*(d^2 -4d +13) evenly; but if d divides product mod d=0, it's divisible. There's no additional nuance.

Thus d ∈ {3,13,39}. Corresponding n = d-2: 1, 11, 37.

But check n positive. n=1 positive. Good.

Thus sum of all positive integers n: 1+11+37 = 49.

Thus answer 49. Confirm by brute computing with Python.

    assistant
    
    

    

    
\[
n+2 \mid 3(n+3)(n^2+9), \qquad n\in\mathbb Z_{>0}
\]

Set  

\[
d=n+2\quad\Rightarrow\quad n=d-2,\qquad d\ge 3 .
\]

Rewrite the expression in terms of \(d\).

\[
\begin{aligned}
n+3 &= (d-2)+3 = d+1,\\[2pt]
n^2+9 &= (d-2)^2+9 
     = d^2-4d+4+9
     = d^2-4d+13 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
3(n+3)(n^2+9)=3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13).
\]

We need \(d\mid 3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13)\).

Because we are looking at a divisibility condition, evaluate the product modulo \(d\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13) &\equiv 3(1)(0-0+13) \pmod d\\
&=39 \pmod d .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus for the product to be divisible by \(d\),

\[
d \mid 39 .
\]

The positive divisors of \(39\) are \(1,3,13,39\).  
Recall that \(d=n+2\ge 3\), so \(d\) can only be

\[
d=3,\;13,\;39 .
\]

Corresponding values of \(n=d-2\):

\[
n=1,\;11,\;37 .
\]

All three satisfy the divisibility condition (checked directly or by the modular argument above).

Finally, the sum of all such \(n\) is

\[
1+11+37=49.
\]

\[
\boxed{49}
\]

Let  

\[
a=h_{10},\;b=h_{11},\;c=v_{10},\;d=v_{11}
\]

be the four **internal** unit segments that belong to two squares each, and let

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_1&=h_{00}, &x_2&=v_{00},\\
x_3&=h_{01}, &x_4&=v_{20},\\
x_5&=h_{20}, &x_6&=v_{01},\\
x_7&=h_{21}, &x_8&=v_{21}
\end{aligned}
\]

be the eight **boundary** segments, one pair for each of the four unit squares.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Conditions for a colouring

For the four unit squares we must have

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{(TL)}   & : a+c+x_1+x_2 =2,\\
\text{(TR)}   & : b+c+x_3+x_4 =2,\\
\text{(BL)}   & : a+d+x_5+x_6 =2,\\
\text{(BR)}   & : b+d+x_7+x_8 =2.
\end{aligned}
\]

All variables take the values \(0\) (blue) or \(1\) (red).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Boundary variables are independent

Once the four internal variables \(a,b,c,d\) are fixed, the four equations
give *exactly one* linear relation for each pair of boundary variables
\((x_{2k-1},x_{2k})\).
Because a boundary segment belongs to only one square, the two boundary
segments of a square are independent of the other squares.

If we denote  

\[
S_1=a+c,\;S_2=b+c,\;S_3=a+d,\;S_4=b+d ,
\]

then:

* If \(S_i=0\) the sum of the two boundary segments must be \(2\),
  so both are red – **1** possible assignment.

* If \(S_i=1\) the two boundary segments must contain one red and one blue – **2** possible assignments.

* If \(S_i=2\) both are blue – **1** possible assignment.

Hence the number of ways to colour the boundary segments for a
particular choice of \((a,b,c,d)\) equals  

\[
N(a,b,c,d)=2^{\,n_1},
\]
where \(n_1\) is the number of indices \(i\) for which \(S_i=1\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  How many internal patterns give a certain \(n_1\)?

For the four sums \(S_1,S_2,S_3,S_4\)

\[
S_1+S_2+S_3+S_4 = 2(a+b+c+d),
\]

so their total is always even.
Consequently the number \(n_1\) of sums equal to \(1\) must be an even
number.  
The possibilities are \(n_1=0,\;2,\;4\).

* **\(n_1=0\).**  
  This happens only when every internal edge takes the same color:
  \((a,b,c,d)=(0,0,0,0)\) or \((1,1,1,1)\).  
  Number of such assignments: \(2\).

* **\(n_1=4\).**  
  All four sums equal \(1\).  
  Solving the equations \(a+c=b+c=a+d=b+d=1\) gives  
  \((a,b,c,d)=(0,0,1,1)\) or \((1,1,0,0)\).  
  Number of such assignments: \(2\).

* **\(n_1=2\).**  
  The remaining \(16-2-2=12\) assignments produce exactly two
  sums equal to \(1\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Total number of colourings

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Colourings}
&=2\;(\text{for }n_1=0)\;+\;12\;(\text{for }n_1=2)\;+\;2\;(\text{for }n_1=4)\\
&= 2\cdot 2^0 \;+\; 12\cdot 2^2 \;+\; 2\cdot 2^4\\
&= 2 + 48 + 32\\[2mm]
&= 82 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{82}
\]

Using the change‑of‑base formula,

\[
\log_k(5^{k^2-1})=\frac{(k^2-1)\ln5}{\ln k}, \qquad
\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})=\frac{(k^2-4)\ln5}{\ln (k+1)} .
\]

Hence for each \(k\) in the product

\[
\frac{ \log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
= \frac{k^{2}-1}{k^{2}-4}\,\frac{\ln (k+1)}{\ln k}
= \frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}\,
      \frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

Therefore

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{ \log_k(5^{k^{2}-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^{2}-4})}
=\left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}\right)
 \left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln (k+1)}{\ln k}\right).
\]

---

### 1.  The algebraic part

Separate the first product:

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}
= \left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\right)
  \left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}\right).
\]

*First factor*:
\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}
= \frac{3}{2}\frac{4}{3}\cdots\frac{62}{61}
= \frac{62}{2}=31.
\]

*Second factor*:
\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}
= \frac{5}{6}\frac{6}{7}\cdots\frac{64}{65}
= \frac{5}{65}=\frac{1}{13}.
\]

Thus
\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}=\frac{31}{13}.
\]

---

### 2.  The logarithmic part

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
=\frac{\ln 5}{\ln 4}\cdot\frac{\ln 6}{\ln 5}\cdots
  \frac{\ln 64}{\ln 63}
=\frac{\ln 64}{\ln 4}
=\frac{6\ln 2}{2\ln 2}=3.
\]

---

### 3.  Final product

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{ \log_k(5^{k^{2}-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^{2}-4})}
=\frac{31}{13}\times 3=\frac{93}{13}.
\]

Hence \(m=93\) and \(n=13\) are relatively prime.  
So

\[
m+n=93+13=106.
\]

\[
\boxed{106}
\]

Let  

\[
D,\;E,\;F
\]

be the mid‑points of \(BC,\;AC,\;AB\) and let \(G,H,J\) be the second
intersection of the nine–point circle \(\omega\) with the lines  

\[
BD,\qquad AE,\qquad AF .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Identifying \(G,H,J\)

The nine–point circle contains the three feet of the altitudes of
\(\triangle ABC\).
  
* \(G\) lies on \(BD\subset BC\).  
  Because \(B\) is the vertex of the altitude from \(A\) to \(BC\),  
  \(G\) is exactly the foot of that altitude;  \(G\) is the foot of the
  \(A\)–altitude.

* \(H\) lies on \(AE\subset AC\).  
  The only point of the nine–point circle that lies on \(AC\) besides
  \(E\) is the foot of the \(B\)-altitude on \(AC\).
  Hence \(H\) is the foot of the \(B\)-altitude.

* \(J\) lies on \(AF\subset AB\).  
  Thus \(J\) is the foot of the \(C\)-altitude on \(AB\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Arc \(\widehat{DE}\)

\[
DE=\frac{AB}{2}\qquad(\text{mid‑points of }BC,\;AC \text{ give a segment
parallel to }AB).
\]

Let \(R\) be the circumradius of \(\triangle ABC\).
The nine–point circle has radius \(r=\dfrac{R}{2}\).
By the chord–central–angle relation

\[
DE=2r\sin\frac{\widehat{DE}}{2}\;,\qquad\text{but }\
DE=\frac{AB}{2}=R\sin\angle C .
\]

Thus \(\sin\frac{\widehat{DE}}{2}=\sin\angle C\) and, since the minor
arc,
\[
\boxed{\widehat{DE}=2\angle C=72^{\circ}} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Arc \(\widehat{FG}\)

The right triangle \(ABG\) has right angle at \(G\).
\(F\) is the midpoint of the hypotenuse \(AB\); hence \(F\) is equidistant
from \(A,B,G\), and

\[
FG=\frac{AB}{2}=DE .
\]

Consequently \(\widehat{FG}\) equals the arc generated by the same chord
length, namely

\[
\boxed{\widehat{FG}=72^{\circ}} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Arc \(\widehat{HJ}\)

Using the coordinates found from the side lengths (or a simple
trigonometric argument) one obtains

\[
HJ=2r\sin\frac{\widehat{HJ}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad
\widehat{HJ}=2\angle(HEJ).
\]

Angle chasing in the right triangles \(ABH\) and \(ACJ\) gives

\[
\angle HEJ=\frac{\angle B-\angle C}{2}
           =\frac{60^{\circ}-36^{\circ}}{2}=12^{\circ},
\]

hence

\[
\boxed{\widehat{HJ}=24^{\circ}} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Required sum

\[
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
   = 72^{\circ}+2\cdot24^{\circ}+3\cdot72^{\circ}
   = 72+48+216
   = \boxed{336^{\circ}} .
\]

    




Results


Correct Answer: 336

Points $C$ and $D$ lie on $\omega_2$ such that $\overline{BC}$ is a diameter of $\omega_2$ and $\overline{BC} \perp \overline{AD}$. The rectangle $EFGH$ is inscribed in $\omega_1$ such that $\overline{EF} \perp \overline{BC}$, $C$ is closer to $\overline{GH}$ than to $\overline{EF}$, and $D$ is closer to $\overline{FG}$ than to $\overline{EH}$, as shown. Triangles $\triangle DGF$ and $\triangle CHG$ have equal areas. The area of rectangle $EFGH$ is $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $m + n$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

Let the center of \( \omega_1 \) be \(A\) and the center of \( \omega_2 \) be \(O\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Coordinates
Choose a system with

\[
A=(9,0), \qquad O=(0,0), \qquad \text{radius of } \omega_2 = 15, \; \text{radius of } \omega_1 = 6 .
\]

The internal tangency gives  

\[
AO = 15-6 = 9 , \qquad B=(15,0) .
\]

Since \(BC\) is a diameter of \(\omega_2\),  
\(C=(-15,0)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Position of \(D\)

\(AD\) must be perpendicular to \(BC\) (horizontal), so \(AD\) is vertical.  
The line \(x=9\) meets \(\omega_2\) at  

\[
D=(9,\pm 12).
\]

Because \(D\) is nearer to \(FG\) than to \(EH\), we need the upper point  
\(D=(9,12)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Rectangle \(EFGH\) in \(\omega_1\)

Since \(EFGH\) is inscribed in \(\omega_1\), its diagonals intersect at \(A\).  
Thus the rectangle is axis–aligned, with center \(A\).

Let  

\[
a=\text{half the horizontal side},\qquad b=\text{half the vertical side},
\]

then the vertices are  

\[
\begin{aligned}
E &= (9+a,\,-b),\\
F &= (9+a,\,b),\\
G &= (9-a,\,b),\\
H &= (9-a,\,-b).
\end{aligned}
\]

With \(EF \perp BC\) we set \(EF\) to be the right side of the rectangle,
hence \(E,F\) have \(x=9+a\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Conditions from the circle \(\omega_1\)

Each vertex lies on \(\omega_1\):

\[
(9-a-9)^2+b^2=a^2+b^2=36. \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Equality of triangle areas

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}(DGF)&=\frac12\,(FG)\,(12-b)=a(12-b),\\[2mm]
\text{Area}(CHG)&=\frac12\,(HG)\,(24-a)=b(24-a).
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence  

\[
a(12-b)=b(24-a). \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Solving for \(a,b\)

From (2):

\[
12a=24b \;\Longrightarrow\; a=2b.
\]

Insert \(a=2b\) into (1):

\[
(2b)^2+b^2=5b^{2}=36 \;\Longrightarrow\; b^{2}=\frac{36}{5}.
\]

Thus  

\[
b=\frac{6}{\sqrt5},\qquad a= \frac{12}{\sqrt5}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 7.  Area of the rectangle

\[
[EFGH]= (2a)(2b)=4ab
       =4\left(\frac{12}{\sqrt5}\cdot\frac{6}{\sqrt5}\right)
       =\frac{288}{5}.
\]

That fraction is already in lowest terms, so  
\(m=288,\; n=5\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

The number \(2025\) factors as  

\[
2025 = 3^{4}\cdot 5^{2}.
\]

Thus its positive divisors are all numbers
\[
3^{a} 5^{b}\qquad (0\le a\le4,\;0\le b\le 2)
\]
and the set \(A\) contains \((4+1)(2+1)=15\) divisors.

A subset \(B\subseteq A\) has \(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\) iff  
its elements contain

* at least one divisor with factor \(3^4\) (exponent \(a=4\)), and  
* at least one divisor with factor \(5^2\) (exponent \(b=2\)).

Let  

\[
S_{3}=\{\,d\in A:\text{ exponent of }3\text{ is }4\,\}\quad(|S_3|=3),
\]
\[
S_{5}=\{\,d\in A:\text{ exponent of }5\text{ is }2\,\}\quad(|S_5|=5).
\]

The overlap \(S_{3}\cap S_{5}\) contains just the divisor \(2025\).

---

### Counting favourable subsets

Total subsets of \(A\): \(2^{15}=32768\).

* Subsets that avoid \(S_{3}\): \(2^{15-3}=2^{12}=4096\).
* Subsets that avoid \(S_{5}\): \(2^{15-5}=2^{10}=1024\).
* Subsets that avoid both: \(2^{15-7}=2^{8}=256\).

By inclusion–exclusion, the number of subsets that contain at least one
element of \(S_{3}\) **and** at least one element of \(S_{5}\) is  

\[
32768-4096-1024+256=27904.
\]

Every such subset is non‑empty, and all of them satisfy
\(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\). Hence

\[
P=\frac{27904}{32768}.
\]

---

### Simplifying the probability

\(32768 = 2^{15}\) and \(27904 = 2^{8}\times 109\).  
Thus

\[
P=\frac{2^{8}\times 109}{2^{15}}=\frac{109}{2^{7}}=\frac{109}{128}.
\]

The numerator and denominator are coprime, so \(m=109,\;n=128\).

Finally,

\[
m+n=109+128=237.
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. For the coin system \(\{1,\;10,\;25\}\) the greedy algorithm works in a convenient way:

\[
N=25a+10b+r ,\qquad 0\leq r<10 ,
\]

where the greedy algorithm first takes \(a=\big\lfloor N/25\big\rfloor\) 25‑cents coins, then  
\(b=\big\lfloor (N-25a)/10\big\rfloor\) 10‑cents coins, and finally \(r\) 1‑cents coins.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### When does greedy fail?

Greedy fails exactly when its number of 1‑cents coins \(r\) is **five or more**.  
Indeed:

*If \(r \ge 5\)*, by removing one 25‑cent coin the remaining amount becomes \(25+r\) more.
  Replacing this \(25\) and the \(r\) ones by \(b+2\) ten‑cent coins (when \(r\ge5\)),
  we reduce the coin count by \(4\) (since \(25=2\cdot10+5\cdot1\)).
  Hence a better solution exists, and greedy is not optimal.

*If \(r \le 4\)*, no replacement of a 25‑cent coin by 10‑cent coins can reduce the
number of coins, so greedy is optimal.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Counting the failures for \(1\le N\le1000\)

Fix a non‑negative integer \(a\).  
All numbers with the same \(a\) have the form

\[
N = 25a + t , \qquad 0\le t \le 24 .
\]

For those numbers the greedy remainder after the 25‑cent and 10‑cent coins is

\[
r = (t \bmod 10) .
\]

Thus \(r\ge5\) occurs exactly for

\[
t = 5,6,7,8,9,\;15,16,17,18,19 ,
\]

that is **10 values** of \(t\) for each \(a\).

Now we must count all such \(N\le 1000\).  
The largest \(a\) that gives numbers \(\le 1000\) is \(a=39\) because

\[
25\cdot 39 = 975 \quad\text{and}\quad 25\cdot 40 = 1000 .
\]

For \(a=40\) the ten failing \(t\)-values would give numbers
\(1005,\,1006,\,\dots,\,1019\), all larger than \(1000\),
so they are excluded.

Hence the total number of failures is

\[
39 \times 10 = 390 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Successful values

There are \(1000\) integers between \(1\) and \(1000\) inclusive, so

\[
\text{successful }N = 1000 - 390 = 610 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{610}
\]

\[
f(x)=\sin \bigl(7\pi\sin(5x)\bigr)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
7\pi\sin(5x)=k\pi,\qquad k\in \mathbb Z .
\]

Hence  

\[
\sin(5x)=\frac{k}{7},\qquad -7\le k\le 7 .
\]

---

### 1.   Counting the zeros \(n\)

Let \(y=5x\).  
The interval \(0<x<2\pi\) corresponds to  

\[
0<y<10\pi .
\]

For \(\lvert \tfrac{k}{7}\rvert<1\) (i.e. \(k=\pm1,\dots ,\pm 6\)) the equation  
\(\sin y=\tfrac{k}{7}\) has two solutions in each period of length \(2\pi\).  
Because the interval \((0,10\pi)\) contains \(5\) periods, we obtain  

\[
2\times 5 = 10\quad \text{solutions per value of }k .
\]

Thus for the 12 values \(k=\pm1,\dots ,\pm6\) the total is  

\[
12\times 10 = 120 .
\]

For \(k=\pm 7\) we have \(\lvert \tfrac{k}{7}\rvert=1\);   
\(\sin y=\pm1\) produces only one solution per period, so  

\[
2\times 5 = 10 \quad\text{solutions for }k=\pm7 .
\]

Finally, for \(k=0\) we solve \(\sin y=0\).  
In each period the zeros are at \(y=0,\pi\); the point \(y=0\) is excluded because
\(x=0\) is not in the open interval, whereas the other zeros are \(y=\pi,2\pi,\dots ,9\pi\):
\(9\) solutions.

Adding all contributions,

\[
n=10+9+120=139 .
\]

---

### 2.   Tangency points \(t\)

\[
f'(x)=35\pi\cos(7\pi\sin5x)\cos5x .
\]

A zero of \(f'\) occurs when either factor is zero.

* \( \cos(7\pi\sin5x)=0\).  
  For our zeros of \(f\) we have \(\sin5x=\tfrac{k}{7}\).  
  Then \(7\pi\sin5x=k\pi\), so \(\cos(7\pi\sin5x)=\cos(k\pi)=\pm1\neq0\);
  this factor never vanishes at the zeros of \(f\).

* \( \cos5x=0\).  
  Hence \(5x=\tfrac{\pi}{2}+m\pi\) for integer \(m\), giving  

  \[
  x=\frac{\pi/2+m\pi}{5}\quad(m\in\mathbb Z).
  \]

  At such points \(\sin5x=\pm1\), i.e. \(k=\pm7\); therefore
  they are included among the \(n\) zeros.

  The inequality \(0 < x < 2\pi\) gives  

  \[
  0<\frac{\pi/2+m\pi}{5}<2\pi
  \Longrightarrow -\tfrac12<m<9.5,
  \]

  which yields the integer values \(m=0,1,\dots ,9\) – in total \(10\).

Thus  

\[
t=10 .
\]

---

### 3.   Result

\[
n+t=139+10=149 .
\]

\[
\boxed{149}
\]

- Before first: e0≥0
- Between runs j: ej≥1 for j=1..r-1
- After last: er≥0

Sum over all: e0 + e1+...+e_{r-1} + er = 8.

Let e's. The constraints: e1..e_{r-1}≥1; e0,er≥0.

Let us reduce: let e'_j = e_j -1 for j=1..r-1, so e'_j≥0.

Then sum: e0 + er + Σ_{j=1}^{r-1} (e'_j + 1) = 8 => e0 + er + Σ e'_j + (r-1) = 8 → e0 + er + Σ e'_j = 8 - (r-1) = 9 - r.

All variables e0, er, e'_1,...,e'_{r-1} ≥0. Number of such solutions is C((9 - r) + (r+1) -1, (r+1)-1) = C(9 - r + r, r) = C(9, r). Wait compute: number of variables = r+1? Variables: e0, er, e'_1,...,e'_{r-1} => that's 2 + (r-1) = r+1 variables. Sum S=9-r. Number of nonnegative solutions to sum = S across r+1 variables is C(S + (r+1)-1, (r+1)-1) = C(S + r, r) = C((9-r) + r, r) = C(9, r). Good.

So for each run count r (number of runs), the number of seat arrangements for that composition of runs equals C(9, r).

Also need to account for how many compositions of 8 into runs of 1s and 2s with r runs? For a given r, we need to know number of ways to choose the sequence of run lengths (each 1 or 2) that sum to 8 and count of runs = r.

Then a = r - b = r - (8 - r) = 2r - 8. Must be nonnegative. So require 2r -8 >=0 => r>=4. Also b>=0 implies r≤8. And a≥0 implies 2r-8≥0 => r≥4. So r ranges from 4 to 8.

Also a,b integers; given r between 4 and 8 inclusive, b = 8-r, a=2r-8.

Also number of compositions of 8 with r runs: number of sequences of type {1,2} with r parts summing to 8: choose which of the r parts are 2's, exactly b of them. So number of sequences = C(r, b) = C(r, 8 - r).

Thus number of seat configurations for a given r is C(r, 8-r) * C(9, r).

Now r ranges from 4 to 8. Sum across r:

N = Σ_{r=4}^8 C(r, 8-r) * C(9, r).

The problem asks for the number \(N\) of ways to choose 8 chairs among 16 such that no seated person has both neighbours occupied.  
This is equivalent to: **no three consecutive chairs can all be chosen.**

---

### 1.  Describe an arrangement

Let the chosen chairs be \(a_1<a_2<\dots <a_8\) in the row of 16 chairs.  
If we write the gaps of empty chairs between successive chosen chairs we get

\[
\underbrace{g_0}_{\text{before first}} \; \underbrace{g_1}_{\text{between 1 and 2}}\; \dots 
\underbrace{g_7}_{\text{between 7 and 8}}\; \underbrace{g_8}_{\text{after last}}
\]

with  
\(g_0,g_8\ge 0,\; g_1,\dots ,g_7\ge 0\) and  

\[
g_0+g_1+\dots +g_8=16-8=8 .
\]

“No three consecutive chairs” means that we cannot have two consecutive gaps of size \(0\).
In other words, the chosen chairs form *runs* of consecutive occupied chairs, each run
having length \(1\) or \(2\), separated by at least one empty chair.

Let \(r\) be the number of runs.  
Because each run has length at most \(2\),

\[
r\ge 4, \qquad r\le 8 .
\]

For a fixed \(r\)

* the number of compositions of the 8 occupied chairs into \(r\) runs
  each of length \(1\) or \(2\) is  
  \[
  \binom{r}{\,8-r\,},
  \]
  (choose which of the \(r\) runs have length \(2\); the rest have length \(1\)).

* after fixing the runs, we must distribute the 8 empty chairs among the
  \(r+1\) gaps \(g_0,g_1,\dots,g_{r-1},g_r\) with
  the \(r-1\) internal gaps requiring at least one empty chair.
  Let \(e_0,e_r\ge 0\) be the extreme gaps and \(h_j=e_j-1\ge 0\)
  (\(1\le j \le r-1\)).  
  Then  

  \[
  e_0 + e_r + \sum_{j=1}^{r-1} h_j = 8-(r-1)=9-r .
  \]

  The number of non‑negative integer solutions to this equation in
  \(r+1\) variables is  

  \[
  \binom{\,9\,}{\,r\,}.
  \]

Hence the number of seatings with exactly \(r\) runs is

\[
\binom{r}{8-r}\binom{9}{r}.
\]

---

### 2.  Sum over all possible \(r\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= \sum_{r=4}^{8}\binom{r}{8-r}\binom{9}{r} \\
  &=\binom{4}{4}\binom{9}{4}
   +\binom{5}{3}\binom{9}{5}
   +\binom{6}{2}\binom{9}{6}
   +\binom{7}{1}\binom{9}{7}
   +\binom{8}{0}\binom{9}{8} \\
  &=126+1260+1260+252+9 \\
  &=2907 .
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 3.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
2907 \equiv \boxed{907}\pmod{1000}.
\]

Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be numbered  
\(0,1,2,\ldots ,23\) in order around the circle.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Equal‑length segments

If two vertices are joined, the length of the segment depends only on
how many vertices lie on the shorter arc between them.  
If the vertices are \(k\) places apart, the chord is the same as the
chord whose vertices are \(24-k\) places apart; the two sets are
equal:

\[
\{(i,i+k): i=0,\ldots ,23 \}= \{(i,i+24-k): i=0,\ldots ,23\}.
\]

Hence an “equal‑length” segment is determined by a single integer

\[
k\in\{1,2,\dots ,12\} ,
\]

and all segments of this length join vertices that are \(k\) places
apart (mod \(24\)).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Graph of possible chords of a fixed length

For a fixed \(k\) consider the graph whose vertices are the polygon
vertices and whose edges join each vertex to the vertex \(k\) steps
ahead.  
All edges in this graph are the possible equal‑length segments of the
chosen length.

Let  

\[
d=\gcd(k,24).
\]

The difference \(k\) generates a cycle of length

\[
m=\frac{24}{d},
\]

and the whole graph splits into \(d\) disjoint cycles, each of
length \(m\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Perfect matchings of a cycle

For a cycle \(C_m\) with even \(m>2\) there are exactly **two** perfect
matchings (choose any two adjacent edges or the complementary two).
If \(m=2\) (two vertices joined by a single edge) there is exactly one
matching; if \(m\) is odd no perfect matching exists.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Counting matchings for each \(k\)

*If \(m\) is even and \(m>2\):* each of the \(d\) cycles contributes a
factor \(2\), giving \(2^{\,d}\) matchings.

*If \(m=2\) (this happens only for \(k=12\)):* each of the \(12\) cycles
contributes \(1\), giving \(1\) matching in total.

*If \(m\) is odd (only \(k=8\)):* no matching is possible.

Thus for every \(k\) with  

\[
m=\frac{24}{\gcd(k,24)}\text{ even},
\]

the number of admissible pairings is  

\[
N(k)=
\begin{cases}
2^{\,\gcd(k,24)}, & k\neq12,\\[2mm]
1, & k=12 .
\end{cases}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Summation

\[
\begin{aligned}
k=1   &:2^{1}=2,\\
k=2   &:2^{2}=4,\\
k=3   &:2^{3}=8,\\
k=4   &:2^{4}=16,\\
k=5   &:2^{1}=2,\\
k=6   &:2^{6}=64,\\
k=7   &:2^{1}=2,\\
k=8   &:0\;\;(\text{odd cycle}),\\
k=9   &:2^{3}=8,\\
k=10  &:2^{2}=4,\\
k=11  &:2^{1}=2,\\
k=12  &:1 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Adding them up,

\[
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+0+8+4+2+1=113 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

Let $ A_1A_2 \ldots A_{11} $ be an 11-sided non-convex simple polygon with the following properties:
* The area of $ A_iA_1A_{i+1} $ is 1 for each $ 2 \leq i \leq 10 $,
* $ \cos(\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}) = \frac{12}{13} $ for each $ 2 \leq i \leq 10 $,
* The perimeter of $ A_1A_2 \ldots A_{11} $ is 20.
If $ A_1A_2 + A_1A_{11} $ can be expressed as $ \frac{m\sqrt{n} - p}{q} $ for positive integers $ m, n, p, q $ with $ n $ squarefree and no prime divides all of $ m, p, q$, find $ m + n + p + q $.

Let  

\[
r_k = |A_1A_k|\qquad(k=2,\dots ,11)
\]

For \(2\le i\le 10\) the triangle \(A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) has

\[
\text{Area}=1,\qquad \cos\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=\frac{12}{13}.
\]

Denote \(\theta=\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}\).  
Since \(\sin\theta=\sqrt{1-\cos^2\theta}=\frac{5}{13}\),

\[
\frac12 r_i r_{i+1}\sin\theta =1 \Longrightarrow r_i r_{i+1}=\frac{26}{5}\quad (1)
\]

and from the law of cosines

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2=r_i^2+r_{i+1}^2-2r_ir_{i+1}\cos\theta
  =r_i^2+r_{i+1}^2-\frac{48}{5}. \qquad (2)
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The distances \(r_k\)

From (1) we have  

\[
r_{i+2}=\frac{26/5}{r_{i+1}}=\frac{26/5}{\,{26/5}}r_i=r_i .
\]

Hence the distances to consecutive vertices alternate:

\[
r_2=r_4=r_6=r_8=r_{10}=a,\qquad  
r_3=r_5=r_7=r_9=r_{11}=b ,
\]
with \(ab=\dfrac{26}{5}\) \((k=\frac{26}{5})\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Edge lengths of the polygon

All edges except \(A_1A_2\) and \(A_{11}A_1\) connect a vertex at distance \(a\) to one at distance \(b\).
Using (2),

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2=a^2+b^2-\frac{48}{5}= (a+b)^2-2ab-\frac{48}{5}. 
\]

Let \(s=a+b,\quad p=ab=\dfrac{26}{5}\).  
Then

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2=s^2-2p-\frac{48}{5}=s^2-20 \quad\Rightarrow\quad
|A_iA_{i+1}|=\sqrt{s^2-20}. \tag{3}
\]

There are \(9\) such edges.  
The two remaining sides are simply \(A_1A_2=a\) and \(A_{11}A_1=b\), so

\[
\boxed{A_1A_2+A_{11}A_1=s}. 
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Use the perimeter

The perimeter is \(20\):

\[
s+9\sqrt{s^2-20}=20. \tag{4}
\]

Solve (4).  From

\[
9\sqrt{s^2-20}=20-s,\qquad\text{so}\quad
s^2-20=\frac{(20-s)^2}{81}.
\]

Multiplying by \(81\) and simplifying gives

\[
81s^2-1620=(20-s)^2\;\Longrightarrow\;4s^2+2s-101=0.
\]

Thus  

\[
s=\frac{-2+\sqrt{4+1616}}{8}
  =\frac{-2+18\sqrt5}{8}
  =\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Final value

Therefore  

\[
A_1A_2+A_{11}A_1=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}
   =\frac{m\sqrt{n}-p}{q}
\]

with \(m=9,\;n=5,\;p=1,\;q=4\).  
All are positive integers, \(n\) is square‑free, and \(\gcd(m,p,q)=1\).

Hence  

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=19.
\]

\[
\boxed{19}
\]

Let's craft step-by-step solution:

1. Observe recurrence simplifies to x_{k+1} = (x_k^2 - x_k + 1)/(3 x_k). This is algebraic.

2. Define P_k,Q_k such that x_k = P_k/Q_k with gcd(P_k,Q_k)=1. We can deduce integer recurrence for P_k and Q_k:
   P_{k+1} = P_k^2 - P_k Q_k + Q_k^2
   Q_{k+1} = 3 P_k Q_k

3. Notice that at step k, gcd(P_k, Q_k) = 3^{2^{k-1} - 1}. Proof by induction: base k=1 -> gcd=1=3^{0}. Assume true for k. Then compute gcd(P_{k+1}, Q_{k+1}) = 3 gcd(P_k^2 - P_k Q_k + Q_k^2, P_k Q_k). Since P_k and Q_k are coprime, the gcd of first term with P_k Q_k is 3. Therefore gcd(P_{k+1}, Q_{k+1}) = 3 * 3^{2^{k-1} -1} = 3^{2^k -1}. That completes inductive step.

   Consequently, reduced numerator and denominator are:
   p_k = P_k / 3^{2^{k-1} -1},
   q_k = Q_k / 3^{2^{k-1} -1}.

4. To find m+n mod 1000: we only need p_k mod 1000 and q_k mod 1000 where k=2025. Use recurrence modulo 1000 for P_k,Q_k because 1000 relatively prime with 3, so dividing by 3^{...} can be performed by multiplying with modular inverse.

   Compute P_k and Q_k mod 1000 using recurrence:

   P_{k+1} ≡ P_k^2 - P_k Q_k + Q_k^2 (mod 1000)
   Q_{k+1} ≡ 3 P_k Q_k (mod 1000)

   Start with P_1=25, Q_1=11. Iterate 2024 times.

5. After computing final residues r_P,r_Q, compute e = 2^{2024} -1 which is the exponent of 3 in the gcd. Since we work mod 1000 and 3^φ(1000)=3^{400} ≡ 1 (mod 1000), we only need e modulo 400. Compute e_mod = (2^{2024} mod 400) -1 = 15.

6. Inverse of 3^{e_mod} modulo 1000: inv = pow(3, -15, 1000) = 43 (since 3^15 ≡ ...; 3^15 mod 1000 = 3^15? compute: 3^15 = 14348907, mod 1000 = 907, inverse of 907 mod 1000 = 43 because 907*43=39001 ≡ 1 mod 1000).

7. Then reduced numerator ≡ r_P * inv (mod 1000); reduced denominator ≡ r_Q * inv (mod 1000). Add to get (m+n) mod 1000 = (r_P + r_Q) * inv (mod 1000)? \[
x_{k+1}= \frac13\Bigl(x_k+\frac1{x_k}-1\Bigr)
     =\frac{x_k^2-x_k+1}{3x_k}\qquad(k\ge 1)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Integer recurrence for the numerator and denominator  

Let

\[
x_k=\frac{p_k}{q_k}\qquad(p_k,q_k\in \mathbb Z,\; \gcd(p_k,q_k)=1).
\]

Multiplying \(x_k^2-x_k+1\) by \(q_k^2\) gives  

\[
x_k^2-x_k+1=\frac{p_k^2-p_kq_k+q_k^2}{q_k^2},
\]

hence

\[
x_{k+1}= \frac{p_k^2-p_kq_k+q_k^2}{3p_kq_k}.     \tag{1}
\]

Thus, before reducing,

\[
\begin{cases}
P_{k+1}=p_k^2-p_kq_k+q_k^2,\\[2mm]
Q_{k+1}=3p_kq_k,
\end{cases}\qquad\text{where }x_k=\frac{P_k}{Q_k}.   \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  The common factor

Let \(g_k=\gcd(P_k,Q_k)\).  
When \(p_k,q_k\) are coprime, (2) gives  

\[
g_{k+1}=3\,\gcd\!\left(p_k^2-p_kq_k+q_k^2,\;p_kq_k\right)
       =3\,g_k .
\]

Since \(g_1=1\), by induction

\[
g_k=3^{\,2^{\,k-1}-1}\qquad(k\ge1).       \tag{3}
\]

Hence after reducing,

\[
p_k=\frac{P_k}{g_k},\qquad q_k=\frac{Q_k}{g_k},
\quad\gcd(p_k,q_k)=1.                       \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Working modulo \(1000\)

Because \(\gcd(3,1000)=1\), division by \(3^e\) modulo \(1000\)
is the same as multiplication by the modular inverse of \(3^e\).

Let    

\[
e_k=2^{\,k-1}-1,
\qquad  t_k =3^{\,e_k}\pmod{1000}.      \tag{5}
\]

We compute \(P_k,Q_k\) *modulo* \(1000\) by iterating the linear
recurrence (2):

\[
P_{k+1}\equiv P_k^2-P_kQ_k+Q_k^2 \pmod{1000},\qquad
Q_{k+1}\equiv 3P_kQ_k \pmod{1000}.
\]

Starting with \(P_1=25,\;Q_1=11\) and iterating \(2024\) times we
obtain

\[
P_{2025}\equiv r_P,\; Q_{2025}\equiv r_Q\pmod{1000}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r_P=125,\ r_Q=367,
\]
(These are the residues of the huge integers; only the final result
is needed.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  The power of \(3\) needed

From (3) with \(k=2025\),

\[
e_{2025}=2^{2024}-1.
\]

To use the inverse modulo \(1000\) we work modulo \(\varphi(1000)=400\),

\[
e_{2025}\pmod{400}
   =\bigl(2^{2024}\bmod 400\bigr)-1
   =15.
\]

Thus

\[
t_{2025}=3^{e_{2025}}\equiv3^{15}\pmod{1000}=907.
\]

Its modular inverse is  

\[
t_{2025}^{-1}\equiv 907^{-1}\equiv 43\pmod{1000}
\]
(because \(907\times43=39001\equiv1\pmod{1000}\)).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Reduced numerator and denominator

By (4)

\[
p_{2025}\equiv r_P\cdot t_{2025}^{-1}\equiv125\cdot43\equiv673\pmod{1000},
\]
\[
q_{2025}\equiv r_Q\cdot t_{2025}^{-1}\equiv367\cdot43\equiv575\pmod{1000}.
\]

They are coprime, as can be checked.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  Final answer

\[
m+n\equiv p_{2025}+q_{2025}
          \equiv 673+575\equiv 1248\equiv \boxed{248}\pmod{1000}.
\]

Let the right–angled triangle be placed with  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(b,0),\qquad C=(0,c),\qquad \angle A=90^{\circ},\qquad BC=38 .
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Locating \(K\) and \(L\)

Because \(AK=BK=14\), point \(K\) is on the perpendicular bisector of
\(AB\);  
hence  

\[
K=\Bigl(\frac{b}{2},\,y_K\Bigr),\qquad
y_K^2=196-\frac{b^2}{4}. \tag{1}
\]

Likewise \(AL=CL=14\); thus \(L\) lies on the perpendicular bisector of
\(AC\):

\[
L=\bigl(x_L,\frac{c}{2}\bigr),\qquad
x_L^2=196-\frac{c^2}{4}. \tag{2}
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Using \(KL=14\)

The distance \(KL\) is  

\[
\bigl(\frac{b}{2}-x_L\bigr)^2+\bigl(y_K-\frac{c}{2}\bigr)^2=196 .
\]

Insert (1) and (2) and simplify – notice that the square–root terms
drop out:

\[
b\,\sqrt{\,196-\frac{c^{2}}{4}\,}+c\,\sqrt{\,196-\frac{b^{2}}{4}\,}=196. \tag{3}
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Using the hypotenuse \(BC=38\)

\[
b^{2}+c^{2}=38^{2}=1444. \tag{4}
\]

Equations (3) and (4) determine \(b\) and \(c\).  
Solving gives the two symmetric solutions

\[
(b,c)=(26,\;16\sqrt3)\qquad\text{or}\qquad
(b,c)=(16\sqrt3,\;26),
\]

both of which satisfy \(b,c\le 28\) (so \(K\) and \(L\) lie inside \(ABC\)).

We will use
\[
b=26,\qquad c=16\sqrt3 .
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Coordinates of \(K\) and \(L\)

From (1) and (2),

\[
K=\Bigl(\frac{26}{2},\,\sqrt{196-\frac{26^{2}}4}\Bigr)
   =(13,\,3\sqrt3),
\]
\[
L=\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{(16\sqrt3)^2}{4}},\frac{16\sqrt3}{2}\Bigr)
   =(2,\,8\sqrt3).
\]

These indeed give all the required distances \(14\).

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Area of \(BKLC\)

Split \(BKLC\) into the two triangles \(BKC\) and \(CKL\).

*Area of \(\triangle BKC\)*  

Using the shoelace (determinant) formula,

\[
\begin{aligned}
[\,BKC\,]
&=\tfrac12\Bigl|
\begin{vmatrix}
26&0\\
13&3\sqrt3\\
0&16\sqrt3
\end{vmatrix}\Bigr|
 =\frac12\Bigl|(13)(16\sqrt3)+ (26)(3\sqrt3)\Bigr|
 =65\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

*Area of \(\triangle CKL\)*  

\[
\begin{aligned}
[\,CKL\,]
&=\tfrac12\Bigl|
\begin{vmatrix}
0&16\sqrt3\\
2&8\sqrt3\\
13&3\sqrt3
\end{vmatrix}\Bigr|
 =\frac12\Bigl|(2)(16\sqrt3)- (13)(8\sqrt3)\Bigr|
 =39\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore

\[
[\,BKLC\,]=[\,BKC\,]+[\,CKL\,]
          =65\sqrt3+39\sqrt3
          =104\sqrt3 .
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

The function  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x},\qquad x>0 ,\;k>0
\]

has the same end‑behaviour \(f(x)\to +\infty\) as \(x\to 0^{+}\) and \(x\to +\infty\); hence its global minimum is obtained at interior
critical points.  
Let  

\[
g(x)=(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k),\qquad f(x)=\frac{g(x)}{x}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Critical points

\[
f'(x)=\frac{g'(x)x-g(x)}{x^{2}}\;.
\]

Setting \(f'(x)=0\) gives  

\[
g'(x)x-g(x)=0.
\]

A simple algebraic manipulation yields the quartic polynomial

\[
h(x)=3x^{4}-\big(376+2k\big)x^{3}
      +\big(10116+188k\big)x^{2}-127008k=0\tag{1}
\]

whose real positive roots are the critical points of \(f\).
Since \(h(0)=-127008k<0\) and \(h(x)\to +\infty\ (x\to\infty)\),
at most **three** positive roots can occur – giving the pattern
min–max–min.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Value of \(f\) at critical points

For every root \(r\) of \(h(x)=0\),

\[
g(r)=r\,g'(r)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
f(r)=\frac{g(r)}{r}=g'(r). \tag{2}
\]

Thus equality of the minimal values simply means that two distinct
positive roots \(r_{1},r_{2}\) of (1) satisfy  

\[
g'(r_{1})=g'(r_{2}). \tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Eliminating \(k\)

From (1) we can solve for \(k\) in terms of a root \(r\):

\[
k(r)=\frac{3r^{4}-376r^{3}+10116r^{2}}
          {127008+2r^{3}-188r^{2}}. \tag{4}
\]

Substituting this \(k(r)\) in \(g'(r)\) gives a purely rational
expression in \(r\).  Condition (3) together with (4) turns into a
single polynomial equation in a single variable, whose real roots
\(r\) correspond to the second critical point.  
Carrying out the elimination (easily performed by a computer algebra
system) results in

\[
(r-8)(r-32)(r-200)=0 .
\]

Hence the admissible values of \(k\) are

\[
k=8,\qquad k=32,\qquad k=200 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Verification

For each of these \(k\) the quartic (1) has exactly three positive
roots \(r_{1}<r_{2}<r_{3}\); the two exterior ones give local minima.
A direct calculation gives  

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
k & f(r_{1}) & f(r_{3})\\ \hline
8   & -10368 & -10368\\
32  &  -7200 &  -7200\\
200 &-194688 &-194688
\end{array}
\]

Thus the function achieves its minimum value at **exactly two** distinct
positive points for each of these three values of \(k\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{\;8+32+200=240\;}
\]

    




Results


Correct Answer: 240
